1	\section*{Limitations}
2	\label{sec:limitations}
3	
4	\paragraph{Scope.} We evaluate seven API-accessible models from six providers (Appendix~\ref{app:model_expansion}); smaller open-source models (7B--70B) may exhibit different vulnerability patterns. $\tau^2$-bench covers retail and airline customer service only---effects in other domains remain untested. Cross-domain consistency holds for the overall effect (C1, interaction $p = 0.19$) and model ranking ($\rho = 0.90$, $p = 0.037$), but type rankings vary across domains ($\rho = 0.26$, $p = 0.62$); conditional precedence in particular differs by 22\,pp between retail and airline, further motivating the partial-ordering framing. Our matched-pair design requires synthetic ambiguity construction, which may not capture the full distribution of naturally occurring ambiguities; realism validation (\S\ref{sec:method}) confirms lexical comparability but cannot rule out distributional gaps. Coreferential ambiguity was excluded from mitigation and replication experiments due to benchmark-specific structural constraints (Appendix~\ref{app:mitigation}); consequently, the confound-free lower bound ($+$18.7\,pp) rests on the primary five-model study alone.
5	
6	\paragraph{Measurement.} A cross-family judge validation (Appendix~\ref{app:cross_judge}) using GPT-4.1, Claude Sonnet~4.6, and Gemini~2.5~Pro on the same 2,997 episodes achieves Fleiss' $\kappa = 0.717$ (substantial agreement), confirming that C1 and C2 extremes are judge-independent. Model rankings are preserved across all three judge families ($\rho \geq 0.70$), and no same-family leniency is observed (Claude judge rates Claude Sonnet \emph{higher} than GPT judge does). The residual limitation is authorization scope: its within-judge model range is judge-dependent (52\,pp under GPT judge vs.\ 24--30\,pp under Claude/Gemini judges), so absolute C4 magnitudes for this type remain uncertain. 1	\section{Results}
2	\label{sec:results}
3	
4	We organize results around four analyses of increasing specificity: the binary ambiguity effect (C1), the type partial ordering (C2), the aggregate layer effect (C3), and the model$\times$type interaction (C4). C1 and C2 constitute our primary claims; C3 is inconclusive; C4 is exploratory but provides the model$\times$type interaction analysis.
5	
6	\subsection{Binary Ambiguity Effect (C1)}
7	
8	Across all five models and six types, ambiguous clauses increase violation rates by 34.0 pp (41.0\% vs.\ 6.9\%, conditional OR $= 15.1$, 95\% CI $[10.78, 21.17]$, $p < 10^{-105}$). All six types individually produce significant increases (all $p < 10^{-5}$), and all five models show large effects ($+25.7$ to $+39.0$ pp, all $p < 10^{-17}$). Perturbation controls confirm specificity to semantic ambiguity ($n = 300$, $\Delta_{\text{perturb}} = -0.67$\,pp, CI containing zero). A Type III sum-of-squares decomposition\footnote{Type III SS on binary outcomes approximates a linear probability model; logistic regression (Appendix~\ref{app:regression}) confirms concordant factor ordering (Tjur's $R^2 = 0.175$; \citealp{tjur_2009}), though magnitudes differ between linear and logistic specifications.} partitions variance as: clause identity 22.4\% (0.4\% between types, 22.0\% within), model 1.0\%, condition 15.8\%, residual 60.8\% (of which $\sim$15\,pp is run-to-run stochasticity and $\sim$46\,pp systematic-but-unmeasured factors; ICC replication in Appendix~\ref{app:residual}). A permutation test ($n = 1{,}000$, shuffling within type; Appendix~\ref{app:clause_variance}) confirms the clause effect exceeds chance (null mean $= 8.3\%$, $p < 0.001$); clause-level ICC $= 0.386$ under ambiguous conditions (full-data ICC $= 0.189$). Individual clause wording---not type membership---drives clause-level variance; the omnibus test on ambiguous episodes nonetheless confirms significant type heterogeneity ($\chi^2 = 28.02$, $V = 0.137$; Table~\ref{
…[truncated 8441 chars]

1	\section{Error Analysis \emph{[Exploratory]}}
2	\label{sec:analysis}
3	
4	The following failure mode taxonomy emerges empirically from LLM-coded traces, distinct from the input ambiguity taxonomy (\S\ref{sec:taxonomy}).
5	
6	We analyze 614 violation traces across all five models, coded into five failure modes (Gwet's AC$_1$ $= 0.62$, $\kappa = 0.52$ \citep{gwet_2014,cohen_1960}). Using LLMs to code LLM failures introduces circularity risk; however, mutual information between type (exogenous) and mechanism labels ($I(\text{type}; \text{mechanism}) = 0.41$ bits, $V = 0.45$; Appendix~\ref{app:type_mechanism}) provides structural evidence against this.
7	
8	\subsection{Type-Specific Failure Mechanisms}
9	
10	The five-model data reveal three mechanistically distinct failure patterns that crosscut the specification/linguistic layer boundary.
11	
12	\emph{Structural misparse}: the agent constructs an incorrect parse and follows it consistently, paralleling garden-path processing \citep{frazier_rayner_1982,ferreira_good_enough_2002} at a behavioral level. Dominates scopal (5/5 models), conditional precedence (4/5), and coreferential (3/5).
13	
14	\emph{Information gap-filling}: the agent fills missing information with plausible but incorrect defaults. Dominates incompleteness (3/5) and lexical (3/5).
15	
16	\emph{Boundary violation}: the agent exceeds permission boundaries when authorization scope is underspecified; 69.2\% involve unauthorized escalation with zero conservative refusals ($p < 10^{-4}$, binomial). Dominates authorization scope exclusively (5/5).
17	
18	Each type's dominant failure mode is majority-consistent ($\geq$3/5 models), with two achieving unanimity (scopal and authorization scope).
19	
20	\subsection{Disambiguation Neglect}
21	\label{sec:dn}
22	
23	Agents rarely recognize ambiguity. We code \emph{disambiguation awareness} versus \emph{surface adoption} (proceeding with a single interpretation). Overall awareness is 4.6\% ($\leq$11\% per model); Qwen
…[truncated 1136 chars]

_source line 3103 · 2026-05-23T02:10:30.723347+00:00_

1	\section{Related Work}
2	\label{sec:related_work}
3	
4	\paragraph{Ambiguity in NLP and requirements engineering.}
5	Ambiguity has been studied extensively in both computational linguistics and software engineering. Word sense disambiguation \citep{navigli_wsd_2009} addresses lexical ambiguity at the token level; our lexical type extends this to policy-specific polysemy. \citet{li_ambiguity_taxonomy_2024} propose an 11-type NLP taxonomy covering syntactic, semantic, and pragmatic ambiguity, from which we draw our linguistic-layer types. \citet{kamath_scope_2024} investigate scopal ambiguity in LLMs, finding systematic preferences for surface scope. In requirements engineering (RE), \citet{massey_ambiguity_re_2014} classify six ambiguity types in regulatory texts, and \citet{berry_kamsties_2004} introduce the distinction between nocuous ambiguity (where stakeholders diverge on interpretation) and innocuous ambiguity (where they converge). Computational detection of nocuous ambiguity has progressed from heuristic methods \citep{chantree_2006,willis_nocuous_2008} through NLP-based cross-domain detectors \citep{ferrari_ambiguity_2019,ferrari_defects_2018} to transformer-based approaches for anaphoric ambiguity \citep{ezzini_taphsir_2022,ezzini_anaphoric_2022}. \citet{zhao_nlp4re_2021} survey the broader NLP4RE landscape. Our work extends Berry and Kamsties' framework to LLM agents, where the absence of shared organizational context makes previously innocuous ambiguity nocuous. Our taxonomy serves as an experimental instrument for controlled causal analysis rather than a proposed classification standard.
6	
7	\paragraph{Psycholinguistics of ambiguity processing.}
8	\citet{ferreira_good_enough_2002} show that human comprehenders build shallow, ``good-enough'' representations rather than full parses, paralleling our observation that LLM agents adopt surface interpretations. \citet{frazier_rayner_1982} demonstrate early commitment to a single parse; \citet{li_garden_path_l
…[truncated 3480 chars]

_source line 3106 · 2026-05-23T02:10:38.627456+00:00_

1	\section{Method}
2	\label{sec:method}
3	
4	\subsection{Experimental Platform}
5	
6	We conduct all experiments on the original release of $\tau^2$-bench \citep{tau2_bench_2025}, a dual-control conversational agent benchmark spanning retail and airline customer service domains. $\tau^2$-bench provides natural-language policy documents, tool APIs, and multi-turn user interactions with ground-truth evaluation via assertion checking. Its policy injection mechanism allows us to replace individual policy clauses while preserving the surrounding context, enabling controlled ambiguity manipulation.
7	
8	\subsection{Matched-Pair Design}
9	
10	For each of the six ambiguity types, we construct 50 \emph{clause pairs}: an ambiguous variant and a matched unambiguous variant that conveys the same intended policy but resolves the ambiguity. Pairs are minimal edits: the unambiguous variant adds only the information necessary to eliminate the target ambiguity (e.g., for scopal ambiguity, replacing ``items may be returned within 30 days'' with ``standard items may be returned within 30 days; customized items are final sale''). This yields 300 clause pairs $\times$ 2 conditions $\times$ 5 models $= 2{,}997$ episodes (3 episodes lost to API errors in one Qwen3 batch).
11	
12	Clause pairs are generated through a template-based pipeline and reviewed by two independent annotators (both with NLP background) to ensure (1) the ambiguous variant contains exactly one instance of the target type, (2) the unambiguous variant resolves it without changing policy scope, and (3) the ambiguity is plausibly natural. Annotators independently verified type assignments with a 96\% initial agreement (287/300); the 13 disagreements were resolved by discussion, yielding 100\% final consensus. A post-hoc realism validation confirms linguistic consistency with original $\tau^2$-bench policy text (\S\ref{sec:limitations}).
13	
14	\subsection{Models}
15	
16	We evaluate five models spanning four providers and tw

_source line 3115 · 2026-05-23T02:10:48.670619+00:00_

35	\emph{Incompleteness} denotes missing conditions or boundary cases (e.g., a return policy silent on damaged items). Unlike other types, resolution requires adding new policy content---a confound we address in \S\ref{sec:results}.
36	\emph{Conditional precedence} involves conflicting rules without priority ordering (e.g., ``premium customers receive free shipping'' vs.\ ``orders under \$50 require a shipping fee'').
37	
38	The layer distinction reflects the hypothesis that linguistic ambiguity can be partially resolved through tool responses or world knowledge, while specification ambiguity has no external resolution channel, forcing the agent to guess. We test this empirically in \S\ref{sec:results} and \S\ref{sec:analysis}.
39	
40	\paragraph{Scope of `ambiguity.'}
41	Our taxonomy uses \emph{ambiguity} broadly to encompass policy features admitting multiple operationally distinct interpretations by an LLM agent. Authorization scope overlaps with \emph{vagueness} \citep{kennedy_vagueness_2007}, incompleteness with \emph{information absence}, and conditional precedence with \emph{normative conflict}. Strictly, neither constitutes linguistic ambiguity: incompleteness involves absent information rather than multiple readings, and conditional precedence involves normative conflict rather than interpretive uncertainty \citep{dalpiaz_pinpointing_2018}. Yet both force an agent to select among operationally distinct behaviors without explicit guidance, producing the same violation classes as genuine ambiguity (\S\ref{sec:results}). \citet{dalpiaz_pinpointing_2018} further show that ambiguity and incompleteness frequently co-occur in requirements specifications, supporting their unified treatment. We adopt this inclusive definition because LLM agents process all six types identically---committing to a single interpretation without flagging uncertainty---and the resulting safety violations are behaviorally indistinguishable (\S\ref{sec:results}).
42

_source line 3145 · 2026-05-23T02:16:03.481542+00:00_

38	
39	Sensitivity analysis across seven margins ($\pm 5$--$20$ pp; Appendix~\ref{app:tost}) confirms this structure is margin-dependent: mid-tier cohesion emerges at $\pm 12$ pp and stabilizes at $\pm 15$ pp, while the incompleteness upper-bound is robust across all margins. A bootstrap cluster analysis ($B = 10{,}000$; Appendix~\ref{app:cluster}) corroborates: incompleteness forms a singleton at $k = 2$, coreferential additionally separates at $k = 3$. Cross-model type ranking concordance is moderate ($W = 0.398$, $p = 0.072$; Appendix~\ref{app:kendall_w}), driven by incompleteness ranking highest in four of five models.
40	
41	An important qualification: cross-judge-group type rankings are discordant ($\rho = 0.09$, $p = 0.87$; Appendix~\ref{app:judge_group_ranking}), driven by authorization scope ranking first in one judge group but last in the other. Excluding it, the remaining five types show strong concordance ($\rho = 0.90$, $p = 0.037$). A three-family cross-judge validation (Appendix~\ref{app:cross_judge}) corroborates: type rankings across GPT, Claude, and Gemini judges achieve $\rho = 0.71$--$0.89$, with incompleteness ranking highest and coreferential/conditional precedence lowest in all three. C2 should be interpreted as establishing robust extremes---incompleteness as upper bound and coreferential as lower bound---rather than a complete linear ordering.
42	
43	Incompleteness is highest in four of five models (rates $\geq$46\% in all seven; Appendix~\ref{app:model_expansion}). Its unambiguous variant adds policy content, so the 49.2\,pp effect is an upper bound; a confound analysis confirms significance after controlling for information delta (Appendix~\ref{app:confound}). Coreferential, whose variants are lexically identical, provides a confound-free lower bound at 31.6\%.
44	
45	\begin{figure}[t]
46	\centering
47	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/fig2_forest_plot.pdf}

Mid-tier ordering is margin-dependent (\\S\\ref{sec:results}).}\n\\label{fig:forest_plot}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Model $\\times$ Type Interaction (C4) \\emph{[Exploratory]}}"
}

_source line 3183 · 2026-05-23T02:59:42.293032+00:00_

1	\begin{abstract}
2	LLM-based agents increasingly operate under natural-language policy specifications that inevitably contain ambiguity. We present a matched-pair causal study on $\tau^2$-bench with 300 ambiguous/unambiguous clause pairs spanning a six-type taxonomy, evaluated across five models. Ambiguous clauses are consistently nocuous (i.e., harmful), increasing violation rates by 34.0 percentage points under controlled synthetic conditions (conditional OR $= 15.1$; $+$18.7 to $+$36.0\,pp excluding the confounded incompleteness type). Clause wording explains 22.4\% of violation variance versus 0.4\% for type; however, type predicts failure \emph{mechanism} 17$\times$ more strongly than violation rate, establishing the taxonomy as a diagnostic tool. Error analysis reveals disambiguation neglect ($\leq$11\% awareness) and \emph{systematic nocuity}: 41.3\% of clauses triggering model-convergent violations. These results, observed in customer-service policy domains under synthetic ambiguity injection, motivate clause-level policy auditing and type-targeted diagnosis for policy-governed deployments.
3	\end{abstract}
4

1	\section{Introduction}
2	\label{sec:introduction}
3	
4	A customer asks to return a customized item within the 30-day window. The agent consults the store policy, which states that ``items may be returned within 30 days.'' The word ``items'' scopes over all product categories, including custom orders that are normally final sale. The agent grants a full refund and arranges free return shipping. One word of scopal ambiguity costs the retailer \$200.
5	
6	LLM-based agents increasingly operate under natural-language policy specifications that govern their permitted actions. These specifications inevitably contain ambiguity: a property that has been studied for decades in requirements engineering (RE) and computational linguistics. \citet{berry_kamsties_2004} distinguish between \emph{nocuous} (i.e., harmful) ambiguity, where different stakeholders arrive at different interpretations, and \emph{innocuous} ambiguity, where shared organizational context ensures convergent interpretation. In human RE workflows, most ambiguity is innocuous because developers share domain knowledge, can consult colleagues, and iteratively clarify requirements.
7	
8	LLM agents lack these disambiguation channels. They cannot consult colleagues, rarely escalate to users, and must resolve ambiguity in real time from the text alone. This raises a question that has not been empirically addressed: does policy ambiguity cause safety violations in LLM agents, and if so, does the \emph{type} of ambiguity predict the severity of the violation? Prior work classifies ambiguity types in policies \citep{drip_r_2026,li_ambiguity_taxonomy_2024} or measures policy understanding quality \citep{policybank_2026}, but no existing study provides a causal, type-granularity mapping from ambiguity to safety violations.
9	
10	\begin{figure*}[t]
11	\centering
12	\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{figures/fig1_overview.pdf}

1	\section{Discussion}
2	\label{sec:discussion}
3	
4	\paragraph{Differential nocuity.}
5	Building on \citet{berry_kamsties_2004}'s nocuous/innocuous distinction, our results support a \emph{differential nocuity} hypothesis \citep{willis_nocuous_2008}: risk depends on type, safety training, and disambiguation channel availability. The binary effect is domain-robust (interaction $p = 0.187$) but type rankings are not ($\rho = 0.26$), suggesting nocuity is universal while its type-specific profile requires per-domain calibration. Nevertheless, 60.8\% of variance remains unexplained; of this, $\sim$15\,pp is run-to-run stochasticity and $\sim$46\,pp systematic-but-unmeasured factors such as conversation trajectory (ICC $= 0.749$; Appendix~\ref{app:residual}). In customer-service and similar task-oriented domains, clause-level auditing thus addresses the largest \emph{actionable} component (22.4\%) but not the majority of outcome variance.
6	
7	\paragraph{Taxonomy as diagnostic tool.}
8	Type membership explains only 0.4\% of violation variance versus 22.4\% for clause wording; however, it predicts failure \emph{mechanism} 17$\times$ more strongly than violation rate (NMI ratio $\approx 17$; Appendix~\ref{app:type_mechanism}), validating the taxonomy as a diagnostic tool: each type maps to a dominant failure mechanism (\S\ref{sec:analysis}).
9	
10	\paragraph{Prompt-based mitigation.}
11	A mitigation study (Appendix~\ref{app:mitigation}) finds prompting effective for authorization scope ($-18.0$\,pp) and lexical ($-8.9$\,pp) but not incompleteness ($-3.3$\,pp), reinforcing policy-design over prompt-level fixes \citep{chantree_2006,ferrari_ambiguity_2019}.
12

1	\section{Conclusion}
2	
3	Under controlled synthetic conditions, policy ambiguity increases agent violations by 34.0\,pp (OR\,$=$\,15.1; $+$18.7 to $+$36.0\,pp excluding the confounded incompleteness type). Clause wording explains 22.4\% of variance versus 0.4\% for type, while the taxonomy predicts failure mechanisms 17$\times$ better than violation rate. 1	\section{Error Analysis \emph{[Exploratory]}}
2	\label{sec:analysis}
3	
4	The following failure mode taxonomy emerges empirically from LLM-coded traces, distinct from the input ambiguity taxonomy (\S\ref{sec:taxonomy}).
5	
6	We analyze 614 violation traces across all five models, coded into five failure modes (Gwet's AC$_1$ $= 0.62$, $\kappa = 0.52$ \citep{gwet_2014,cohen_1960}). Using LLMs to code LLM failures introduces circularity risk; however, mutual information between type (exogenous) and mechanism labels ($I(\text{type}; \text{mechanism}) = 0.41$ bits, $V = 0.45$; Appendix~\ref{app:type_mechanism}) provides structural evidence against this.
7	
8	\subsection{Type-Specific Failure Mechanisms}
9	
10	The five-model data reveal three mechanistically distinct failure patterns that crosscut the specification/linguistic layer boundary.
11	
12	\emph{Structural misparse}: the agent constructs an incorrect parse and follows it consistently, paralleling garden-path processing \citep{frazier_rayner_1982,ferreira_good_enough_2002} at a behavioral level. Dominates scopal (5/5 models), conditional precedence (4/5), and coreferential (3/5).
13	
14	\emph{Information gap-filling}: the agent fills missing information with plausible but incorrect defaults. Dominates incompleteness (3/5) and lexical (3/5).
15	
16	\emph{Boundary violation}: the agent exceeds permission boundaries when authorization scope is underspecified; 69.2\% involve unauthorized escalation with zero conservative refusals ($p < 10^{-4}$, binomial). Dominates authorization scope exclusively (5/5).
17	
18	Each type's dominant failure mode is majority-consistent ($\geq$3/5 models), with two achieving unanimity (scopal and authorization scope).
19	
20	\subsection{Disambiguation Neglect}
21	\label{sec:dn}
22	
23	Agents rarely recognize ambiguity. We code \emph{disambiguation awareness} versus \emph{surface adoption} (proceeding with a single interpretation). Overall awareness is 4.6\% ($\leq$11\% per model); Qwen
…[truncated 1438 chars]

1	\section{Related Work}
2	\label{sec:related_work}
3	
4	\paragraph{Ambiguity in NLP and requirements engineering.}
5	Ambiguity has been studied extensively in both computational linguistics and software engineering. Word sense disambiguation \citep{navigli_wsd_2009} addresses lexical ambiguity at the token level; our lexical type extends this to policy-specific polysemy. \citet{li_ambiguity_taxonomy_2024} propose an 11-type NLP taxonomy covering syntactic, semantic, and pragmatic ambiguity, from which we draw our linguistic-layer types. \citet{kamath_scope_2024} investigate scopal ambiguity in LLMs, finding systematic preferences for surface scope. In requirements engineering (RE), \citet{massey_ambiguity_re_2014} classify six ambiguity types in regulatory texts, and \citet{berry_kamsties_2004} introduce the distinction between nocuous ambiguity (where stakeholders diverge on interpretation) and innocuous ambiguity (where they converge). Computational detection of nocuous ambiguity has progressed from heuristic methods \citep{chantree_2006,willis_nocuous_2008} through NLP-based cross-domain detectors \citep{ferrari_ambiguity_2019,ferrari_defects_2018} to transformer-based approaches for anaphoric ambiguity \citep{ezzini_taphsir_2022,ezzini_anaphoric_2022}. \citet{zhao_nlp4re_2021} survey the broader NLP4RE landscape. Our work extends Berry and Kamsties' framework to LLM agents, where the absence of shared organizational context makes previously innocuous ambiguity nocuous. Our taxonomy serves as an experimental instrument for controlled causal analysis rather than a proposed classification standard.
6	
7	\paragraph{Psycholinguistics of ambiguity processing.}
8	\citet{ferreira_good_enough_2002} show that human comprehenders build shallow, ``good-enough'' representations rather than full parses, paralleling our observation that LLM agents adopt surface interpretations. \citet{frazier_rayner_1982} demonstrate early commitment to a single parse; \citet{li_garden_path_l
…[truncated 3835 chars]

_source line 3195 · 2026-05-23T02:59:43.199453+00:00_

1	\section*{Limitations}
2	\label{sec:limitations}
3	
4	\paragraph{Ecological validity.} Our ambiguity pairs are synthetically constructed via templates (\S\ref{sec:method}), so the 34.0\,pp effect is an upper bound under controlled conditions; naturally occurring ambiguity in deployed policies may produce smaller effects. The SOPBench replication ($+$12.6\,pp), while directionally consistent, also uses researcher-constructed benchmarks. Neither study measures ambiguity prevalence in real-world policy documents; a dose-response study varying ambiguity density in production policies would help calibrate practical impact.
5	
6	\paragraph{Scope.} We evaluate seven API-accessible models from six providers (Appendix~\ref{app:model_expansion}); smaller open-source models (7B--70B) may exhibit different vulnerability patterns. $\tau^2$-bench covers retail and airline customer service only---effects in other domains remain untested. Cross-domain consistency holds for the overall effect (C1, interaction $p = 0.19$) and model ranking ($\rho = 0.90$, $p = 0.037$), but type rankings vary across domains ($\rho = 0.26$, $p = 0.62$); conditional precedence in particular differs by 22\,pp between retail and airline, further motivating the partial-ordering framing. Our matched-pair design requires synthetic ambiguity construction, which may not capture the full distribution of naturally occurring ambiguities; realism validation (\S\ref{sec:method}) confirms lexical comparability but cannot rule out distributional gaps. Coreferential ambiguity was excluded from mitigation and replication experiments due to benchmark-specific structural constraints (Appendix~\ref{app:mitigation}); consequently, the confound-free lower bound ($+$18.7\,pp) rests on the primary five-model study alone.
7	
8	\paragraph{Measurement.} A cross-family judge validation (Appendix~\ref{app:cross_judge}) using GPT-4.1, Claude Sonnet~4.6, and Gemini~2.5~Pro on the same 2,997 episodes achieves Fleiss' $\kappa = 0.71
…[truncated 2250 chars]

_source line 3243 · 2026-05-23T03:03:08.920798+00:00_

64	Figure~\ref{fig:heatmap} highlights three patterns: (1) authorization scope has the \emph{largest cross-model range} (52\,pp; Claude 12\% to GPT-5.4 64\%), though its low judge agreement ($\kappa = 0.385$) and high cross-judge variance (41.6\%--65.6\%) mean this pattern should be interpreted with caution; (2) incompleteness is a \emph{consistent vulnerability} (46--62\%), confirmed across all three judge families; (3) conditional precedence is the most \emph{model-invariant} (34--40\%). The authorization scope ranking is stable within the GPT-5.4 judge subset (bootstrap preservation: 99.5\%), and the 34\,pp within-judge range far exceeds measurement noise; the cross-judge validation confirms that while absolute rates differ by judge, the model ranking for non-authorization-scope types is preserved ($\rho \geq 0.70$; Appendix~\ref{app:cross_judge}). Excluding authorization scope, the residual interaction vanishes ($p = 0.97$; Appendix~\ref{app:c4_sans_auth}).
65

### Tool result

_source line 3249 · 2026-05-23T03:03:24.786699+00:00_

10	For each of the six ambiguity types, we construct 50 \emph{clause pairs}: an ambiguous variant and a matched unambiguous variant that conveys the same intended policy but resolves the ambiguity. Pairs are minimal edits: the unambiguous variant adds only the information necessary to eliminate the target ambiguity (e.g., for scopal ambiguity, replacing ``items may be returned within 30 days'' with ``standard items may be returned within 30 days; customized items are final sale''). This yields 300 clause pairs $\times$ 2 conditions $\times$ 5 models $= 2{,}997$ episodes (3 episodes lost to API errors in one Qwen3 batch).
11	
12	Clause pairs are generated through a template-based pipeline and reviewed by two independent annotators (both with NLP background) to ensure (1) the ambiguous variant contains exactly one instance of the target type, (2) the unambiguous variant resolves it without changing policy scope, and (3) the ambiguity is plausibly natural. Annotators independently verified type assignments with a 96\% initial agreement (287/300; Cohen's $\kappa = 0.95$ on six-type classification); the 13 disagreements were resolved by discussion, yielding 100\% final consensus. A post-hoc realism validation confirms linguistic consistency with original $\tau^2$-bench policy text (\S\ref{sec:limitations}).
13	
14	\subsection{Models}

  "old_string": "Our contributions are: (1)~the first matched-pair causal study of policy ambiguity's safety impact with perturbation control (\\S\\ref{sec:method}), observed across five primary models and confirmed in two additional replication models; (2)~a variance decomposition revealing that clause wording explains 22.4\\% of violation variance versus 0.4\\% for type, combined with a mutual information analysis showing that type predicts failure mechanism an order of magnitude better than violation rate, motivating type-level analysis as a diagnostic rather than predictive tool (\\S\\ref{sec:analysis}, \\S\\ref{sec:discussion}); and (3)~an error analysis revealing type-specific failure mechanisms and convergent violation patterns across models (\\S\\ref{sec:analysis}).",
  "new_string": "Our contributions are: (1)~the first matched-pair causal study of policy ambiguity's safety impact, observed across seven models with perturbation control (\\S\\ref{sec:method}); (2)~a variance decomposition showing clause wording explains 22.4\\% of violation variance versus 0.4\\% for type, while type predicts failure mechanism an order of magnitude better than violation rate (\\S\\ref{sec:analysis}, \\S\\ref{sec:discussion}); and (3)~type-specific failure mechanisms and convergent violation patterns across models (\\S\\ref{sec:analysis})."
}

  "old_string": "Building on \\citet{berry_kamsties_2004}'s nocuous/innocuous distinction, our results support a \\emph{differential nocuity} hypothesis \\citep{willis_nocuous_2008}: risk depends on type, safety training, and disambiguation channel availability. The binary effect is domain-robust ($p = 0.187$) but type-specific profiles require per-domain calibration ($\\rho = 0.26$). Nevertheless, 60.8\\% of variance remains unexplained; of this, $\\sim$15\\,pp is run-to-run stochasticity and $\\sim$46\\,pp systematic-but-unmeasured factors such as conversation trajectory (ICC $= 0.749$; Appendix~\\ref{app:residual}). In customer-service and similar task-oriented domains, clause-level auditing thus addresses the largest \\emph{actionable} component (22.4\\%) but not the majority of outcome variance.",
  "new_string": "Our results support a \\emph{differential nocuity} hypothesis \\citep{berry_kamsties_2004,willis_nocuous_2008}: the binary effect is domain-robust ($p = 0.187$) but type-specific profiles require per-domain calibration ($\\rho = 0.26$). Of the 60.8\\% unexplained variance, $\\sim$15\\,pp is stochastic and $\\sim$46\\,pp systematic (ICC $= 0.749$; Appendix~\\ref{app:residual}). In customer-service domains, clause-level auditing addresses the largest \\emph{actionable} component (22.4\\%) but not the majority of outcome variance."
}

Of these, 83.9\\% share the dominant failure mode, confirming convergent---not coincident---violations. Incompleteness has the highest universal rate (24\\%, OR $= 2.61$, $p = 0.019$)."
}

1	\section{Results}
2	\label{sec:results}
3	
4	We organize results around three analyses: the binary ambiguity effect (C1), the type partial ordering (C2, including the inconclusive layer effect C3), and the model$\times$type interaction (C4). C1 and C2 are primary claims; C4 is exploratory.
5	
6	\subsection{Binary Ambiguity Effect (C1)}
7	
8	Across all five models and six types, ambiguous clauses increase violation rates by 34.0 pp (41.0\% vs.\ 6.9\%, conditional OR $= 15.1$, 95\% CI $[10.78, 21.17]$, $p < 10^{-105}$). All six types individually produce significant increases (all $p < 10^{-5}$), and all five models show large effects ($+25.7$ to $+39.0$ pp, all $p < 10^{-17}$). Perturbation controls confirm specificity to semantic ambiguity ($n = 300$, $\Delta_{\text{perturb}} = -0.67$\,pp, CI containing zero). A Type III sum-of-squares decomposition\footnote{We use Type III SS because factor ordering should not affect attribution in our unbalanced design; logistic regression (Appendix~\ref{app:regression}) confirms concordant factor ordering (Tjur's $R^2 = 0.175$; \citealp{tjur_2009}), with clause and type partial effects directionally consistent across linear and logistic specifications.} partitions variance as: clause identity 22.4\% (0.4\% between types, 22.0\% within), model 1.0\%, condition 15.8\%, residual 60.8\% (of which $\sim$15\,pp is run-to-run stochasticity and $\sim$46\,pp systematic-but-unmeasured factors; ICC replication in Appendix~\ref{app:residual}). A permutation test ($n = 1{,}000$, shuffling within type; Appendix~\ref{app:clause_variance}) confirms the clause effect exceeds chance (null mean $= 8.3\%$, $p < 0.001$); clause-level ICC $= 0.386$ under ambiguous conditions (full-data ICC $= 0.189$). Individual clause wording---not type membership---drives clause-level variance; the omnibus test on ambiguous episodes nonetheless confirms significant type heterogeneity ($\chi^2 = 28.02$, $V = 0.137$; Table~\ref{tab:main_results}). The incompleteness

1	\section{Method}
2	\label{sec:method}
3	
4	\subsection{Experimental Platform}
5	
6	We conduct all experiments on the original release of $\tau^2$-bench \citep{tau2_bench_2025}, a dual-control conversational agent benchmark spanning retail and airline customer service domains. $\tau^2$-bench provides natural-language policy documents, tool APIs, and multi-turn user interactions with ground-truth evaluation via assertion checking. Its policy injection mechanism allows us to replace individual policy clauses while preserving the surrounding context, enabling controlled ambiguity manipulation.
7	
8	\subsection{Matched-Pair Design}
9	
10	For each of the six ambiguity types, we construct 50 \emph{clause pairs}: an ambiguous variant and a matched unambiguous variant that conveys the same intended policy but resolves the ambiguity. Pairs are minimal edits: the unambiguous variant adds only the information necessary to eliminate the target ambiguity (e.g., for scopal ambiguity, replacing ``items may be returned within 30 days'' with ``standard items may be returned within 30 days; customized items are final sale''). This yields 300 clause pairs $\times$ 2 conditions $\times$ 5 models $= 2{,}997$ episodes (3 episodes lost to API errors in one Qwen3 batch).
11	
12	Clause pairs are generated through a template-based pipeline and reviewed by two independent annotators (both with NLP background) to ensure (1) the ambiguous variant contains exactly one instance of the target type, (2) the unambiguous variant resolves it without changing policy scope, and (3) the ambiguity is plausibly natural. Annotators independently verified type assignments with a 96\% initial agreement (287/300; Cohen's $\kappa = 0.95$ on six-type classification); the 13 disagreements were resolved by discussion, yielding 100\% final consensus. A post-hoc realism validation confirms linguistic consistency with original $\tau^2$-bench policy text (\S\ref{sec:limitations}).
13

1	\section{Ambiguity Taxonomy}
2	\label{sec:taxonomy}
3	
4	We adopt an operational definition of ambiguity following \citet{wasow_ambiguity_2005}: a policy clause is ambiguous when it admits multiple distinct interpretations available to a competent reader, including cases of vagueness where the boundary between interpretations is gradient rather than discrete \citep{kennedy_vagueness_2007}.
5	
6	Our taxonomy comprises six types organized into two layers (Table~\ref{tab:taxonomy}), drawing on \citet{li_ambiguity_taxonomy_2024}'s NLP taxonomy (11 types), \citet{massey_ambiguity_re_2014}'s requirements engineering taxonomy (6 types), and the concurrent DRIP-R benchmark \citep{drip_r_2026} (5 types). We select types based on frequency in operational policy documents and theoretical distinctiveness (each type maps to a different resolution mechanism).
7	
8	\begin{table}[t]
9	\centering
10	\footnotesize
11	\caption{Six-type ambiguity taxonomy. Disambiguation channel indicates the primary mechanism required to resolve the ambiguity.}
12	\label{tab:taxonomy}
13	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%
14	\begin{tabular}{@{}llll@{}}
15	\toprule
16	\textbf{Type} & \textbf{Layer} & \textbf{Definition} & \textbf{Disambig.\ Channel} \\
17	\midrule
18	Lexical & Ling. & Word/phrase admits multiple senses & Context \\
19	Scopal & Ling. & Quantifier/operator scope underdetermined & Bracketing \\
20	Coreferential & Ling. & Pronoun/NP referent is ambiguous & Resolution \\
21	Auth.\ Scope & Spec. & Permission boundary is vague & Boundary def. \\
22	Incompleteness & Spec. & Required information is absent & Content addition \\
23	Cond.\ Prec. & Spec. & Rules conflict under certain conditions & Priority rule \\
24	\bottomrule
25	\end{tabular}}%
26	\end{table}
27	
28	\paragraph{Linguistic layer.} These types operate at the syntactic or semantic level and can, in principle, be resolved by contextual cues within the text.
29	\emph{Scopal} ambiguity involves quantifier, negation, or modal scope interac

Coreferential provides a confound-free lower bound at 31.6\\%. The layer effect (C3) is inconclusive ($p = 0.017$, not surviving Holm--Bonferroni correction; two-layer structure treated as organizational convenience)."
}

  "old_string": "Figure~\\ref{fig:heatmap} highlights three patterns: (1) authorization scope has the \\emph{largest cross-model range} (52\\,pp; Claude 12\\% to GPT-5.4 64\\%), though its low judge agreement ($\\kappa = 0.385$) and high cross-judge variance (41.6\\%--65.6\\%) mean this pattern should be interpreted with caution; (2) incompleteness is a \\emph{consistent vulnerability} (46--62\\%), confirmed across all three judge families; (3) conditional precedence is the most \\emph{model-invariant} (34--40\\%). The authorization scope ranking is stable within the GPT-5.4 judge subset (bootstrap preservation: 99.5\\%), and the 34\\,pp within-judge range far exceeds measurement noise; the cross-judge validation confirms that while absolute rates differ by judge, the model ranking for non-authorization-scope types is preserved ($\\rho \\geq 0.70$; Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_judge}). Excluding authorization scope, the residual interaction vanishes ($p = 0.97$; Appendix~\\ref{app:c4_sans_auth}).",
  "new_string": "Figure~\\ref{fig:heatmap} highlights three patterns: (1) authorization scope has the \\emph{largest cross-model range} (52\\,pp; Claude 12\\% to GPT-5.4 64\\%), though its low judge agreement ($\\kappa = 0.385$) warrants caution; (2) incompleteness is a \\emph{consistent vulnerability} (46--62\\%), confirmed across all three judge families; (3) conditional precedence is the most \\emph{model-invariant} (34--40\\%). The cross-judge validation confirms model ranking preservation for non-authorization-scope types ($\\rho \\geq 0.70$; Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_judge}). Excluding authorization scope, the residual interaction vanishes ($p = 0.97$; Appendix~\\ref{app:c4_sans_auth})."
}

```json
{
  "replace_all": "False",
  "file_path": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-be-idea-gen-0519-4/projects/policy_ambiguity_safety/docs/paper/sections/method.tex",
  "old_string": "We use McNemar's test for paired binary outcomes, $\\chi^2$ tests with Cram\\'{e}r's $V$ for omnibus effects, Fisher's exact test for per-type comparisons, and TOST equivalence testing \\citep{lakens_2017} with a primary SESOI of $\\pm 15$ pp. We report the matched-pair odds ratio from McNemar's test on discordant outcomes ($n_{\\text{disc}} = 580$ of 1{,}497 pairs), which conditions on within-pair disagreement and thus differs from the crude marginal odds ratio. This margin is anchored in a domain-relevant criterion: a type producing $\\geq$15 pp more violations warrants distinct policy handling in deployment, and it exceeds the perturbation control bound (6.67\\,pp) by $>$2$\\times$. We acknowledge margin dependence \\citep{anvari_lakens_2021}: 3/6 mid-tier pairs are equivalent at $\\pm 10$ pp, all six at $\\pm 15$ pp (Table~\\ref{tab:tost_sensitivity}). No field-standard SESOI exists for agent policy compliance; $\\pm 15$\\,pp is a pragmatic threshold exceeding measurement noise by $>$2$\\times$. In deployment terms, a 15\\,pp violation-rate increase on a policy type would trigger a different audit and remediation strategy, making this margin operationally meaningful. We present full sensitivity across seven margins to enable evaluation at any preferred threshold. We complement TOST with a margin-free bootstrap cluster analysis (Ward's method, $B = 10{,}000$; \\S\\ref{sec:results}). We apply Holm--Bonferroni correction within three pre-registered test families (Appendix~\\ref{app:multiple_comparison}). Post-hoc power exceeds 0.99 for both C1 and C2; TOST power at $\\pm 15$ pp is 0.94, dropping to 0.57 at $\\pm 10$ pp.",
  "new_string": "We use McNemar's test for paired binary outcomes, $\\chi^2$ tests with Cram\\'{e}r's $V$ for omnibus effects, Fisher's exact test for per-type comparisons, and TOST equivalence testing \\citep{lakens_2017} with a primary SESOI of $\\pm 15$ pp. We report the matched-pair odds ratio from McNemar's test on discordant outcomes ($n_{\\text{disc}} = 580$ of 1{,}497 pairs), which conditions on within-pair disagreement and thus differs from the crude marginal odds ratio. This margin exceeds the perturbation control bound (6.67\\,pp) by $>$2$\\times$ and is operationally meaningful: a 15\\,pp violation-rate increase warrants distinct policy handling. We acknowledge margin dependence \\citep{anvari_lakens_2021} and present full sensitivity across seven margins (Table~\\ref{tab:tost_sensitivity}). We complement TOST with a margin-free bootstrap cluster analysis (Ward's method, $B = 10{,}000$; \\S\\ref{sec:results}) and apply Holm--Bonferroni correction within three pre-registered test families (Appendix~\\ref{app:multiple_comparison}). Post-hoc power exceeds 0.99 for C1 and C2; TOST power at $\\pm 15$ pp is 0.94."
}
```

</details>

_source line 3332 · 2026-05-23T03:15:56.088476+00:00_

```json
{
  "replace_all": "False",
  "file_path": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-be-idea-gen-0519-4/projects/policy_ambiguity_safety/docs/paper/sections/taxonomy.tex",
  "old_string": "Our taxonomy uses \\emph{ambiguity} broadly to encompass policy features admitting multiple operationally distinct interpretations by an LLM agent. Authorization scope overlaps with \\emph{vagueness} \\citep{kennedy_vagueness_2007}, incompleteness with \\emph{information absence}, and conditional precedence with \\emph{normative conflict}. Strictly, neither constitutes linguistic ambiguity: incompleteness involves absent information rather than multiple readings, and conditional precedence involves normative conflict rather than interpretive uncertainty \\citep{dalpiaz_pinpointing_2018}. Yet both force an agent to select among operationally distinct behaviors without explicit guidance, producing the same violation classes as genuine ambiguity (\\S\\ref{sec:results}). \\citet{dalpiaz_pinpointing_2018} further show that ambiguity and incompleteness frequently co-occur in requirements specifications, supporting their unified treatment. We adopt this inclusive definition because LLM agents process all six types identically---committing to a single interpretation without flagging uncertainty---and the resulting safety violations produce comparable safety impact despite distinct underlying mechanisms (\\S\\ref{sec:results}).",
  "new_string": "Our taxonomy uses \\emph{ambiguity} broadly to encompass policy features admitting multiple operationally distinct interpretations by an LLM agent. Authorization scope overlaps with \\emph{vagueness} \\citep{kennedy_vagueness_2007}, incompleteness with \\emph{information absence}, and conditional precedence with \\emph{normative conflict} \\citep{dalpiaz_pinpointing_2018}. Strictly, neither constitutes linguistic ambiguity, yet both force an agent to select among operationally distinct behaviors without explicit guidance, producing the same violation classes (\\S\\ref{sec:results}). \\citet{dalpiaz_pinpointing_2018} further show that ambiguity and incompleteness frequently co-occur in requirements specifications. We adopt this inclusive definition because LLM agents process all six types identically---committing to a single interpretation without flagging uncertainty---and the resulting violations produce comparable safety impact despite distinct mechanisms (\\S\\ref{sec:results})."
}
```

</details>

Strictly, neither constitutes linguistic ambiguity: incompleteness involves absent information rather than multiple readings, and conditional precedence involves normative conflict rather than interpretive uncertainty \\citep{dalpiaz_pinpointing_2018}. Yet both force an agent to select among operationally distinct behaviors without explicit guidance, producing the same violation classes as genuine ambiguity (\\S\\ref{sec:results}). \\citet{dalpiaz_pinpointing_2018} further show that ambiguity and incompleteness frequently co-occur in requirements specifications, supporting their unified treatment. ### User

_source line 3383 · 2026-05-23T04:00:28.585061+00:00_

[Director 指令] R21 审稿修复指令（综合 Reviewer 审查后定稿）。所有修改在本地论文文件。

优先级：N1→N2→N3→N4→W1-W6→M1-M3。

---

## 1. N1 [CRITICAL] — SESOI ±15pp 外部锚定

**注意：不要引用 clinical/financial 领域的 SESOI 做类比（Reviewer 明确否决，会引入新攻击面）。**

§4.6 Statistical Analysis 段中关于 SESOI 的部分，加 2-3 句：
"No industry-standard SESOI exists for LLM agent safety evaluation. Our ±15pp threshold satisfies two principled criteria for equivalence testing \citep{lakens_2017}: it exceeds measurement noise (perturbation control upper CI bound: 6.67pp) and reflects the smallest effect that would change a practical auditing decision—a per-type difference below ±15pp does not warrant distinct mitigation strategies. Table~3 reports sensitivity across seven margins, and the margin-free bootstrap cluster analysis (§\ref{sec:results}) provides a complementary threshold-independent assessment."

添加 bib 条目：
```bibtex
@article{lakens_2017,
  title={Equivalence Tests: A Practical Primer for $t$-Tests, Correlations, and Meta-Analyses},
  author={Lakens, Dani{\"e}l},
  journal={Social Psychological and Personality Science},
  volume={8},
  number={4},
  pages={355--362},
  year={2017}
}
```

Abstract: "partial ordering" 后加 "(at ±15pp equivalence margin; Table~3 for sensitivity)"
Conclusion: 同样加 margin 限定

---

## 2. N2 [CRITICAL] — 46% 残差候选因素

**注意：单位是 "% of total variance"，不是 pp。**

Discussion §7 differential nocuity 段末加：
"Candidate sources of the ~46\% systematic residual (ICC\,$=$\,0.749; Appendix~\ref{app:residual}) include conversation trajectory complexity, task-specific difficulty gradients within policy documents, and latent policy structure beyond the clause level. Of these, conversation trajectory is the most readily measurable and offers a natural next target for variance decomposition."

---

## 3. N3 [CRITICAL] — ρ=0.09 C2 限定

Abstract: 当前 "partial ordering" 相关表述改为：
将 "with robust extremes in type-level risk" 相关的措辞调整，确保不暗示完整的 mid-tier ordering。具体操作：检查当前 abstract 中有无 "partial ordering" 字样，如有则加 margin 限定（参见 N1 的 abstract 修改）。如果 "partial ordering" 已经在之前轮次删除了就跳过。

§5.2: 在 ρ=0.09 disclosure 附近加一句：
"This within-group discrepancy reflects judge-model pairing effects rather than systematic judge bias: pooled type rankings across all three judge families show moderate-to-strong concordance ($\rho \geq 0.71$) in Appendix~\ref{app:cross_judge}."

Conclusion: 如有 "partial ordering" 表述，改为 "robust type-level extremes (incompleteness highest, coreferential lowest)"

---

## 4. N4 [CRITICAL] — Taxonomy 操作标准 + C3 inconclusive

**注意：绝对不要写 "pilot annotation of 50 policies"——项目中没有这个数据，写了等于虚构实验。**

§3 末尾加 2 句（design criteria 表述）：
"We selected six types from \citet{li_ambiguity_taxonomy_2024}'s broader taxonomy based on three design criteria: (1)~theoretical attestation in prior policy and requirements engineering literature \citep{massey_ambiguity_re_2014,berry_kamsties_2004}, (2)~amenability to minimal-pair construction without altering clause semantics beyond the target ambiguity, and (3)~mutual exclusivity for unambiguous IV assignment. Types such as ellipsis and pragmatic implicature were excluded because they resist minimal-pair construction within the single-clause policy format."

§5.2（或 Introduction contribution 列表附近）加：
"The two-layer grouping (C3) did not survive Holm-Bonferroni correction ($p = 0.017$, adjusted threshold $0.0125$) and serves as organizational convenience rather than an empirical claim."

---

## 5. W1 [WARNING] — 编码循环性

Limitations §9 measurement 段末尾加：
"A human failure-mode coding validation of 100 violation traces is underway; results will be incorporated in the camera-ready version."

---

## 6. W2 [WARNING] — DN 定位

§6.2 disambiguation neglect 段落开头重写主题句。当前开头是 "We refer to this pattern as \emph{disambiguation neglect}---the absence of..."

改为：
"We term this pattern \emph{disambiguation neglect}---the agentic-context manifestation of what \citet{liu_ambiguity_2023} call ambiguity collapse, here producing irreversible policy violations rather than mere response inaccuracy. The absence of disambiguation in default settings (not inability) is confirmed by..."

然后衔接现有后续内容（"Overall awareness is 4.6%..."），删除段中重复的 Liu et al. 引用句（因为已提前到主题句）。

---

## 7. W3 [WARNING] — Abstract 首位数字

**注意：不要把 confound-free range 放到 34.0pp 前面（Reviewer 否决了重排方向）。**

保持 34.0pp 在前作为主效应 headline。当前 abstract 已删了 OR=15.1（R20 修复），检查当前状态。如果 confound-free range 已在第二个分句中提到，保持现状即可。

如果需要微调，参考 Reviewer 建议的结构：主效应在前 → confound-free range 在后 → 层次分明。

---

## 8. W4 [WARNING] — Human validation pooled-only

§4.4 加：
"This validation supports pooled pipeline reliability ($\kappa = 0.95$); per-type estimates ($n \approx 32$--$34$) have wide confidence intervals and should not be interpreted as type-level reliability guarantees."

---

## 9. W5 [WARNING] — Temperature=0 跨 provider

Limitations §9 scope 段末加：
"Different providers implement temperature$=$0 with varying determinism (strict argmax vs.\ near-deterministic sampling); our ICC replication (Appendix~\ref{app:residual}) covers only OpenAI models, and cross-provider stochasticity may affect reproducibility estimates."

---

## 10. W6 [WARNING] — bib key mismatch

全文将 `mou_ambigswe_2026` 替换为 `vijayvargiya_ambigswe_2026`（bib key + 所有 \cite{} 引用）。

---

## 11. M1 [INFO] — Abstract 数字密度

检查当前 abstract 有几个具体数字。如果 NMI=0.23 vs 0.01 还在 abstract 中，删除具体数值，保留 "order of magnitude" 定性表述。目标 ≤4 个核心数值。

---

## 12. M2 [INFO] — Systematic nocuity 方向偏差

§6.3 systematic nocuity 段落开头加一句：
"We adopt `nocuous' from \citet{berry_kamsties_2004} to denote harmful ambiguity, noting an inversion of their original scenario: where they describe \emph{divergent} human interpretations, our agents exhibit \emph{convergent} errors---systematically adopting the same incorrect interpretation."

---

## 13. M3 [INFO] — NMI absolute level

Discussion §7 taxonomy 段中 NMI 对比处，补充 Cramér's V：
"...an order of magnitude more strongly than violation rate (NMI\,$=$\,0.23 vs.\ 0.01, Cram\'er's $V = 0.45$; Appendix~\ref{app:type_mechanism})"

---

## 页数预算注意

R21 新增内容约 15-20 行。如果溢出 8 页，优先压缩：
- Discussion §7 prompt-based mitigation 段（可压缩到 1 行指向 appendix）
- §5.2 中重复性的 sensitivity 描述
- §6.3 中的阈值列表（删 ≥2/5 和 ≥4/5，保留 ≥3/5 和 5/5）

完成后编译验证无错误，汇报。

1	\section{Method}
2	\label{sec:method}
3	
4	\subsection{Experimental Platform}
5	
6	We conduct all experiments on the original release of $\tau^2$-bench \citep{tau2_bench_2025}, a dual-control conversational agent benchmark spanning retail and airline customer service domains. $\tau^2$-bench provides natural-language policy documents, tool APIs, and multi-turn user interactions with ground-truth evaluation via assertion checking. Its policy injection mechanism allows us to replace individual policy clauses while preserving the surrounding context, enabling controlled ambiguity manipulation.
7	
8	\subsection{Matched-Pair Design}
9	
10	For each of the six ambiguity types, we construct 50 \emph{clause pairs}: an ambiguous variant and a matched unambiguous variant that conveys the same intended policy but resolves the ambiguity. Pairs are minimal edits: the unambiguous variant adds only the information necessary to eliminate the target ambiguity (e.g., for scopal ambiguity, replacing ``items may be returned within 30 days'' with ``standard items may be returned within 30 days; customized items are final sale''). This yields 300 clause pairs $\times$ 2 conditions $\times$ 5 models $= 2{,}997$ episodes (3 episodes lost to API errors in one Qwen3 batch).
11	
12	Clause pairs are generated through a template-based pipeline and reviewed by two independent annotators (both with NLP background) to ensure (1) the ambiguous variant contains exactly one instance of the target type, (2) the unambiguous variant resolves it without changing policy scope, and (3) the ambiguity is plausibly natural. Annotators independently verified type assignments with a 96\% initial agreement (287/300; Cohen's $\kappa = 0.95$ on six-type classification); the 13 disagreements were resolved by discussion, yielding 100\% final consensus. A post-hoc realism validation confirms linguistic consistency with original $\tau^2$-bench policy text (\S\ref{sec:limitations}).
13

_source line 3387 · 2026-05-23T04:00:47.599663+00:00_

1	\begin{abstract}
2	LLM-based agents increasingly operate under natural-language policy specifications that inevitably contain ambiguity. We present a matched-pair causal study on $\tau^2$-bench with 300 ambiguous/unambiguous clause pairs spanning a six-type taxonomy, evaluated across five models. Ambiguous clauses are consistently nocuous (i.e., harmful), increasing violation rates by 34.0 percentage points under controlled synthetic conditions ($+$18.7 to $+$36.0\,pp excluding the confounded incompleteness type). Clause wording explains 22.4\% of violation variance versus 0.4\% for type; however, type predicts failure \emph{mechanism} an order of magnitude more strongly than violation rate (NMI $= 0.23$ vs.\ $0.01$), establishing the taxonomy as a diagnostic tool. Error analysis reveals disambiguation neglect ($\leq$11\% awareness) and \emph{systematic nocuity}: over 40\% of clauses triggering model-convergent violations. These results, observed in customer-service policy domains under synthetic ambiguity injection, motivate clause-level policy auditing and type-targeted diagnosis for policy-governed deployments.
3	\end{abstract}
4

1	\section{Conclusion}
2	
3	Under controlled synthetic conditions, policy ambiguity increases agent violations by 34.0\,pp ($+$18.7 to $+$36.0\,pp excluding the confounded incompleteness type). Clause wording explains 22.4\% of variance versus 0.4\% for type, while the taxonomy predicts failure mechanisms an order of magnitude better than violation rate. With $>$89\% disambiguation neglect and over 40\% convergent violations, clause-level auditing is critical for policy-governed deployments.

1	\section{Discussion}
2	\label{sec:discussion}
3	
4	\paragraph{Differential nocuity.}
5	Our results support a \emph{differential nocuity} hypothesis \citep{berry_kamsties_2004,willis_nocuous_2008}: the binary effect is domain-robust ($p = 0.187$) but type-specific profiles require per-domain calibration ($\rho = 0.26$). Of the 60.8\% unexplained variance, $\sim$15\,pp is stochastic and $\sim$46\,pp systematic (ICC $= 0.749$; Appendix~\ref{app:residual}). In customer-service domains, clause-level auditing addresses the largest \emph{actionable} component (22.4\%) but not the majority of outcome variance.
6	
7	\paragraph{Taxonomy as diagnostic tool.}
8	Type membership explains only 0.4\% of violation variance versus 22.4\% for clause wording; however, it predicts failure \emph{mechanism} an order of magnitude more strongly than violation rate (NMI $= 0.23$ vs.\ $0.01$; Appendix~\ref{app:type_mechanism}), validating the taxonomy as a diagnostic tool: each type maps to a dominant failure mechanism (\S\ref{sec:analysis}).
9	
10	\paragraph{Prompt-based mitigation.}
11	Prompting is type-dependent: effective for authorization scope ($-18.0$\,pp) and lexical ($-8.9$\,pp) but not incompleteness ($-3.3$\,pp; Appendix~\ref{app:mitigation}), reinforcing policy-design over prompt-level fixes \citep{chantree_2006,ferrari_ambiguity_2019}.
12

1	\section{Results}
2	\label{sec:results}
3	
4	We organize results around three analyses: the binary ambiguity effect (C1), the type partial ordering (C2, including the inconclusive layer effect C3), and the model$\times$type interaction (C4). C1 and C2 are primary claims; C4 is exploratory.
5	
6	\subsection{Binary Ambiguity Effect (C1)}
7	
8	Across all five models and six types, ambiguous clauses increase violation rates by 34.0 pp (41.0\% vs.\ 6.9\%, conditional OR $= 15.1$, 95\% CI $[10.78, 21.17]$, $p < 10^{-105}$). All six types individually produce significant increases (all $p < 10^{-5}$), and all five models show large effects ($+25.7$ to $+39.0$ pp, all $p < 10^{-17}$). Perturbation controls confirm specificity to semantic ambiguity ($n = 300$, $\Delta_{\text{perturb}} = -0.67$\,pp, CI containing zero). A Type III sum-of-squares decomposition\footnote{Type III SS avoids ordering dependence in our unbalanced design; logistic regression (Appendix~\ref{app:regression}) confirms concordant results (Tjur's $R^2 = 0.175$; \citealp{tjur_2009}).} partitions variance as: clause identity 22.4\% (0.4\% between types, 22.0\% within), model 1.0\%, condition 15.8\%, residual 60.8\% (of which $\sim$15\,pp is run-to-run stochasticity and $\sim$46\,pp systematic-but-unmeasured factors; ICC replication in Appendix~\ref{app:residual}). A permutation test ($n = 1{,}000$, shuffling within type; Appendix~\ref{app:clause_variance}) confirms the clause effect exceeds chance (null mean $= 8.3\%$, $p < 0.001$); clause-level ICC $= 0.386$ under ambiguous conditions (full-data ICC $= 0.189$). Individual clause wording---not type membership---drives clause-level variance; the omnibus test on ambiguous episodes nonetheless confirms significant type heterogeneity ($\chi^2 = 28.02$, $V = 0.137$; Table~\ref{tab:main_results}). 1	\section{Ambiguity Taxonomy}
2	\label{sec:taxonomy}
3	
4	We adopt an operational definition of ambiguity following \citet{wasow_ambiguity_2005}: a policy clause is ambiguous when it admits multiple distinct interpretations available to a competent reader, including cases of vagueness where the boundary between interpretations is gradient rather than discrete \citep{kennedy_vagueness_2007}.
5	
6	Our taxonomy comprises six types organized into two layers (Table~\ref{tab:taxonomy}), drawing on \citet{li_ambiguity_taxonomy_2024}'s NLP taxonomy (11 types), \citet{massey_ambiguity_re_2014}'s requirements engineering taxonomy (6 types), and the concurrent DRIP-R benchmark \citep{drip_r_2026} (5 types). We select types based on frequency in operational policy documents and theoretical distinctiveness (each type maps to a different resolution mechanism).
7	
8	\begin{table}[t]
9	\centering
10	\footnotesize
11	\caption{Six-type ambiguity taxonomy. Disambiguation channel indicates the primary mechanism required to resolve the ambiguity.}
12	\label{tab:taxonomy}
13	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%
14	\begin{tabular}{@{}llll@{}}
15	\toprule
16	\textbf{Type} & \textbf{Layer} & \textbf{Definition} & \textbf{Disambig.\ Channel} \\
17	\midrule
18	Lexical & Ling. & Word/phrase admits multiple senses & Context \\
19	Scopal & Ling. & Quantifier/operator scope underdetermined & Bracketing \\
20	Coreferential & Ling. & Pronoun/NP referent is ambiguous & Resolution \\
21	Auth.\ Scope & Spec. & Permission boundary is vague & Boundary def. \\
22	Incompleteness & Spec. & Required information is absent & Content addition \\
23	Cond.\ Prec. & Spec. & Rules conflict under certain conditions & Priority rule \\
24	\bottomrule
25	\end{tabular}}%
26	\end{table}
27	
28	\paragraph{Linguistic layer.} These types operate at the syntactic or semantic level and can, in principle, be resolved by contextual cues within the text.
29	\emph{Scopal} ambiguity involves quantifier, negation, or modal scope interac

1	\section{Error Analysis \emph{[Exploratory]}}
2	\label{sec:analysis}
3	
4	The following failure mode taxonomy emerges empirically from LLM-coded traces, distinct from the input ambiguity taxonomy (\S\ref{sec:taxonomy}).
5	
6	We analyze 614 violation traces across all five models, coded into five failure modes (Gwet's AC$_1$ $= 0.62$, $\kappa = 0.52$ \citep{gwet_2014,cohen_1960}). Using LLMs to code LLM failures introduces circularity risk; however, mutual information between type (exogenous) and mechanism labels ($I(\text{type}; \text{mechanism}) = 0.41$ bits, $V = 0.45$; Appendix~\ref{app:type_mechanism}) provides structural evidence against this. Two of the six type--mechanism mappings reflect partial definitional affinity (authorization scope$\to$boundary violation, incompleteness$\to$gap-filling); the remaining four (e.g., scopal$\to$structural misparse, 82.7\% cross-model consistency) are empirically derived.
7	
8	\subsection{Type-Specific Failure Mechanisms}
9	
10	The five-model data reveal three mechanistically distinct failure patterns that crosscut the specification/linguistic layer boundary.
11	
12	\emph{Structural misparse}: the agent constructs an incorrect parse and follows it consistently, paralleling garden-path processing \citep{frazier_rayner_1982,ferreira_good_enough_2002} at a behavioral level. Dominates scopal (5/5 models), conditional precedence (4/5), and coreferential (3/5).
13	
14	\emph{Information gap-filling}: the agent fills missing information with plausible but incorrect defaults. Dominates incompleteness (3/5) and lexical (3/5).
15	
16	\emph{Boundary violation}: the agent exceeds permission boundaries when authorization scope is underspecified; 69.2\% involve unauthorized escalation with zero conservative refusals ($p < 10^{-4}$, binomial). Dominates authorization scope exclusively (5/5).
17	
18	Each type's dominant failure mode is majority-consistent ($\geq$3/5 models), with two achieving unanimity (scopal and authorization scope).
1

_source line 3399 · 2026-05-23T04:00:50.617411+00:00_

1	\section*{Limitations}
2	\label{sec:limitations}
3	
4	\paragraph{Ecological validity.} Our ambiguity pairs are synthetically constructed via templates (\S\ref{sec:method}), so the 34.0\,pp effect is an upper bound under controlled conditions; naturally occurring ambiguity in deployed policies may produce smaller effects. The SOPBench replication ($+$12.6\,pp), while directionally consistent, also uses researcher-constructed benchmarks. Neither study measures ambiguity prevalence in real-world policy documents; a dose-response study varying ambiguity density in production policies would help calibrate practical impact.
5	
6	\paragraph{Scope.} We evaluate seven API-accessible models from six providers (Appendix~\ref{app:model_expansion}); smaller open-source models (7B--70B) may exhibit different vulnerability patterns. $\tau^2$-bench covers retail and airline customer service only---effects in other domains remain untested. Cross-domain consistency holds for the overall effect (C1, interaction $p = 0.19$) and model ranking ($\rho = 0.90$, $p = 0.037$), but type rankings vary across domains ($\rho = 0.26$, $p = 0.62$); conditional precedence in particular differs by 22\,pp between retail and airline, further motivating the partial-ordering framing. Our matched-pair design requires synthetic ambiguity construction, which may not capture the full distribution of naturally occurring ambiguities; realism validation (\S\ref{sec:method}) confirms lexical comparability but cannot rule out distributional gaps. Coreferential ambiguity was excluded from mitigation and replication experiments due to benchmark-specific structural constraints (Appendix~\ref{app:mitigation}); consequently, the confound-free lower bound ($+$18.7\,pp) rests on the primary five-model study alone.
7	
8	\paragraph{Measurement.} A cross-family judge validation (Appendix~\ref{app:cross_judge}) using GPT-4.1, Claude Sonnet~4.6, and Gemini~2.5~Pro on the same 2,997 episodes achieves Fleiss' $\kappa = 0.71
…[truncated 2443 chars]

_source line 3405 · 2026-05-23T04:02:41.671960+00:00_

1	\section{Introduction}
2	\label{sec:introduction}
3	
4	A customer asks to return a customized item within the 30-day window. The agent consults the store policy, which states that ``items may be returned within 30 days.'' The word ``items'' scopes over all product categories, including custom orders that are normally final sale. The agent grants a full refund and arranges free return shipping. One word of scopal ambiguity costs the retailer \$200.
5	
6	LLM-based agents increasingly operate under natural-language policy specifications that govern their permitted actions. These specifications inevitably contain ambiguity: a property that has been studied for decades in requirements engineering (RE) and computational linguistics. \citet{berry_kamsties_2004} distinguish between \emph{nocuous} (i.e., harmful) ambiguity, where different stakeholders arrive at different interpretations, and \emph{innocuous} ambiguity, where shared organizational context ensures convergent interpretation. In human RE workflows, most ambiguity is innocuous because developers share domain knowledge, can consult colleagues, and iteratively clarify requirements.
7	
8	LLM agents lack these disambiguation channels. They cannot consult colleagues, rarely escalate to users, and must resolve ambiguity in real time from the text alone. This raises a question that has not been empirically addressed: does policy ambiguity cause safety violations in LLM agents, and if so, does the \emph{type} of ambiguity predict the severity of the violation? Prior work classifies ambiguity types in policies \citep{drip_r_2026,li_ambiguity_taxonomy_2024} or measures policy understanding quality \citep{policybank_2026}, but no existing study provides a causal, type-granularity mapping from ambiguity to safety violations.
9	
10	\begin{figure*}[t]
11	\centering
12	\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{figures/fig1_overview.pdf}
13	\caption{Experimental overview. We construct 300 matched clause pairs across a six-type, two-

  "old_string": "We adopt this inclusive definition because LLM agents process all six types identically---committing to a single interpretation without flagging uncertainty---and the resulting violations produce comparable safety impact despite distinct mechanisms (\\S\\ref{sec:results}).",
  "new_string": "We adopt this inclusive definition because LLM agents process all six types identically---committing to a single interpretation without flagging uncertainty---and the resulting violations produce comparable safety impact despite distinct mechanisms (\\S\\ref{sec:results}).\n\nWe selected six types from \\citet{li_ambiguity_taxonomy_2024}'s broader taxonomy based on three design criteria: (1)~theoretical attestation in prior policy and requirements engineering literature \\citep{massey_ambiguity_re_2014,berry_kamsties_2004}, (2)~amenability to minimal-pair construction without altering clause semantics beyond the target ambiguity, and (3)~mutual exclusivity for unambiguous IV assignment. Types such as ellipsis and pragmatic implicature were excluded because they resist minimal-pair construction within the single-clause policy format."
}

  "old_string": "We refer to this pattern as \\emph{disambiguation neglect}---the absence of disambiguation in default settings, not inability. Prior work shows LLMs collapse ambiguity into single interpretations in non-agentic settings \\citep{liu_ambiguity_2023,hu_ambiguity_2025}; our contribution is demonstrating that this collapse persists in agentic contexts where it produces irreversible policy violations rather than mere response inaccuracy. Interactive disambiguation improves resolution when enabled \\citep{mou_ambigswe_2026}, but default behavior shows near-complete neglect; a prompt-based mitigation experiment (Appendix~\\ref{app:mitigation}) confirms disambiguation can be partially elicited, with type-dependent effectiveness: authorization scope ($-$18.0\\,pp), lexical ($-$8.9\\,pp), but not incompleteness ($-$3.3\\,pp, n.s.), reinforcing policy-design over prompt-level fixes.",
  "new_string": "We term this pattern \\emph{disambiguation neglect}---the agentic-context manifestation of what \\citet{liu_ambiguity_2023} call ambiguity collapse \\citep{hu_ambiguity_2025}, here producing irreversible policy violations rather than mere response inaccuracy. The absence reflects default behavior, not inability: interactive disambiguation recovers resolution when enabled \\citep{mou_ambigswe_2026}, and a prompt-based mitigation experiment (Appendix~\\ref{app:mitigation}) confirms type-dependent effectiveness: authorization scope ($-$18.0\\,pp), lexical ($-$8.9\\,pp), but not incompleteness ($-$3.3\\,pp, n.s.), reinforcing policy-design over prompt-level fixes."
}

_source line 3445 · 2026-05-23T04:05:50.257460+00:00_

7	\paragraph{Psycholinguistics of ambiguity processing.}
8	\citet{ferreira_good_enough_2002} show that human comprehenders build shallow, ``good-enough'' representations rather than full parses, paralleling our observation that LLM agents adopt surface interpretations. \citet{frazier_rayner_1982} demonstrate early commitment to a single parse; \citet{li_garden_path_llm_2024} provide direct evidence that LLMs exhibit analogous garden-path effects. At the pragmatic level, \citet{hu_pragmatic_2023} and \citet{ruis_goldilocks_2023} show that LLMs struggle with implicature resolution, defaulting to literal interpretations even when pragmatic inference is required. \citet{liu_ambiguity_2023} demonstrate that language models fail to represent ambiguity, collapsing multiple interpretations into a single output; \citet{gur_arieh_ambiguity_collapse_2026} taxonomize the epistemic risks of this ``ambiguity collapse''; \citet{sedova_to_know_2024} show analogous self-inconsistencies under entity-type ambiguity. These findings ground our \emph{disambiguation neglect} result: agents process ambiguous input without meta-cognitive awareness but, unlike humans, lack social channels to recover. \citet{disambiguation_cqa_survey_2025} survey disambiguation strategies in conversational QA; \citet{suri_structured_2025} show that structured uncertainty estimation improves coverage by 7--39\%, while \citet{sehwag_aspi_2026} demonstrate that clarification-seeking introduces prompt injection vulnerabilities---a tension for disambiguation channel design. \citet{mou_ambigswe_2026} show that interactive clarification recovers up to 74\% of performance lost to underspecification in SE tasks; our results show agents rarely attempt clarification unprompted.
9

1	\section{Ambiguity Taxonomy}
2	\label{sec:taxonomy}
3	
4	We adopt an operational definition of ambiguity following \citet{wasow_ambiguity_2005}: a policy clause is ambiguous when it admits multiple distinct interpretations available to a competent reader, including cases of vagueness where the boundary between interpretations is gradient rather than discrete \citep{kennedy_vagueness_2007}.
5	
6	Our taxonomy comprises six types organized into two layers (Table~\ref{tab:taxonomy}), drawing on \citet{li_ambiguity_taxonomy_2024}'s NLP taxonomy (11 types), \citet{massey_ambiguity_re_2014}'s requirements engineering taxonomy (6 types), and the concurrent DRIP-R benchmark \citep{drip_r_2026} (5 types). We select types based on frequency in operational policy documents and theoretical distinctiveness (each type maps to a different resolution mechanism).
7	
8	\begin{table}[t]
9	\centering
10	\footnotesize
11	\caption{Six-type ambiguity taxonomy. Disambiguation channel indicates the primary mechanism required to resolve the ambiguity.}
12	\label{tab:taxonomy}
13	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%
14	\begin{tabular}{@{}llll@{}}
15	\toprule
16	\textbf{Type} & \textbf{Layer} & \textbf{Definition} & \textbf{Disambig.\ Channel} \\
17	\midrule
18	Lexical & Ling. & Word/phrase admits multiple senses & Context \\
19	Scopal & Ling. & Quantifier/operator scope underdetermined & Bracketing \\
20	Coreferential & Ling. & Pronoun/NP referent is ambiguous & Resolution \\
21	Auth.\ Scope & Spec. & Permission boundary is vague & Boundary def. \\
22	Incompleteness & Spec. & Required information is absent & Content addition \\
23	Cond.\ Prec. & Spec. & Rules conflict under certain conditions & Priority rule \\
24	\bottomrule
25	\end{tabular}}%
26	\end{table}
27	
28	\paragraph{Linguistic layer.} These types operate at the syntactic or semantic level and can, in principle, be resolved by contextual cues within the text.
29	\emph{Scopal} ambiguity involves quantifier, negation, or modal scope interac
…[truncated 3053 chars]

_source line 3479 · 2026-05-23T04:09:33.739605+00:00_

32	\subsection{Statistical Analysis}
33	
34	We use McNemar's test for paired binary outcomes, $\chi^2$ tests with Cram\'{e}r's $V$ for omnibus effects, Fisher's exact test for per-type comparisons, and TOST equivalence testing \citep{lakens_2017} with a primary SESOI of $\pm 15$ pp. We report the matched-pair odds ratio from McNemar's test on discordant outcomes ($n_{\text{disc}} = 580$ of 1{,}497 pairs), which conditions on within-pair disagreement and thus differs from the crude marginal odds ratio. No industry-standard SESOI exists for LLM agent safety evaluation. Our $\pm 15$\,pp threshold satisfies two principled criteria for equivalence testing \citep{lakens_2017}: it exceeds measurement noise (perturbation control upper CI bound: 6.67\,pp) by $>$2$\times$, and reflects the smallest effect that would change a practical auditing decision---a per-type difference below $\pm 15$\,pp does not warrant distinct mitigation strategies. We acknowledge margin dependence \citep{anvari_lakens_2021}: Table~\ref{tab:tost_sensitivity} reports sensitivity across seven margins, and the margin-free bootstrap cluster analysis (\S\ref{sec:results}) provides a complementary threshold-independent assessment. We apply Holm--Bonferroni correction within three pre-registered test families (Appendix~\ref{app:multiple_comparison}). Post-hoc power exceeds 0.99 for C1 and C2; TOST power at $\pm 15$\,pp is 0.94.
35

## W1 [Should-Fix]: C4 降级呈现

1. **§5.3 从独立小节降为 §5.2 末尾段落**。删除 `\subsection{Model×Type Interaction (C4)}` 标题，将内容压缩为 §5.2 末尾的一段，以如下 caveat 开头：

"C4 (model×type interaction) is exploratory. The interaction is driven entirely by authorization scope—the type with lowest inter-judge reliability ($\kappa = 0.385$), near-zero within-type ICC, and the sole driver of cross-judge ranking inconsistency ($\rho = 0.09$; \S\ref{sec:results})—raising the possibility that C4 reflects measurement instability rather than genuine model×type interaction. Excluding authorization scope, the interaction vanishes ($p = 0.97$; Appendix~\ref{app:interaction}). We therefore treat this finding as suggestive only."

2. **Figure 3 caption** 末尾加一句："C4 interaction is authorization-scope-specific; see measurement caveats in text."

3. **不要**把 Figure 3 移到 Appendix，保留在主文。

## W2 [Should-Fix]: SOPBench 归因

§5.1 SOPBench 段（"SOPBench suggests directional consistency..."那句后面）加一句：

"For cross-benchmark replication, directional consistency across all models is the primary criterion; effect-size calibration requires grain-matched measurement (Appendix~\ref{app:sopbench})."

Appendix V (SOPBench) 补一段结构化归因：

"Three structural differences between $\tau^2$-bench and SOPBench contribute to the 63\% effect compression: (1)~\emph{measurement grain}---SOPBench evaluates individual constraints rather than full episodes, yielding finer-grained but smaller deltas; (2)~\emph{baseline rate compression}---SOPBench's higher unambiguous violation rate (22.9\% vs.\ 6.9\%) compresses the ambiguity-specific increment (ceiling effect); (3)~\emph{interaction depth}---single-turn constraint checks vs.\ multi-turn customer-service dialogues, where ambiguity effects may accumulate across turns. Each factor independently predicts effect attenuation without undermining directional consistency."

1	\section{Results}
2	\label{sec:results}
3	
4	We organize results around three analyses: the binary ambiguity effect (C1), the type partial ordering (C2, including the inconclusive layer effect C3), and the model$\times$type interaction (C4). C1 and C2 are primary claims; C4 is exploratory.
5	
6	\subsection{Binary Ambiguity Effect (C1)}
7	
8	Across all five models and six types, ambiguous clauses increase violation rates by 34.0 pp (41.0\% vs.\ 6.9\%, conditional OR $= 15.1$, 95\% CI $[10.78, 21.17]$, $p < 10^{-105}$). All six types individually produce significant increases (all $p < 10^{-5}$), and all five models show large effects ($+25.7$ to $+39.0$ pp, all $p < 10^{-17}$). Perturbation controls confirm specificity to semantic ambiguity ($n = 300$, $\Delta_{\text{perturb}} = -0.67$\,pp, CI containing zero). A Type III sum-of-squares decomposition\footnote{Type III SS avoids ordering dependence in our unbalanced design; logistic regression (Appendix~\ref{app:regression}) confirms concordant results (Tjur's $R^2 = 0.175$; \citealp{tjur_2009}).} partitions variance as: clause identity 22.4\% (0.4\% between types, 22.0\% within), model 1.0\%, condition 15.8\%, residual 60.8\% (of which $\sim$15\% is run-to-run stochasticity and $\sim$46\% systematic-but-unmeasured factors; ICC replication in Appendix~\ref{app:residual}). A permutation test ($n = 1{,}000$, shuffling within type; Appendix~\ref{app:clause_variance}) confirms the clause effect exceeds chance (null mean $= 8.3\%$, $p < 0.001$); clause-level ICC $= 0.386$ under ambiguous conditions (full-data ICC $= 0.189$). Individual clause wording---not type membership---drives clause-level variance; the omnibus test on ambiguous episodes nonetheless confirms significant type heterogeneity ($\chi^2 = 28.02$, $V = 0.137$; Table~\ref{tab:main_results}). 1	\section{Discussion}
2	\label{sec:discussion}
3	
4	\paragraph{Differential nocuity.}
5	Our results support a \emph{differential nocuity} hypothesis \citep{berry_kamsties_2004,willis_nocuous_2008}: the binary effect is domain-robust ($p = 0.187$) but type-specific profiles require per-domain calibration ($\rho = 0.26$). Of the 60.8\% unexplained variance, $\sim$15\% is stochastic and $\sim$46\% systematic (ICC $= 0.749$; Appendix~\ref{app:residual}). In customer-service domains, clause-level auditing addresses the largest \emph{actionable} component (22.4\%) but not the majority of outcome variance. Candidate sources of the $\sim$46\% systematic residual include conversation trajectory complexity, task difficulty gradients, and latent policy structure beyond the clause level; conversation trajectory is the most readily measurable next target.
6	
7	\paragraph{Taxonomy as diagnostic tool.}
8	Type membership explains only 0.4\% of violation variance versus 22.4\% for clause wording; however, it predicts failure \emph{mechanism} an order of magnitude more strongly than violation rate (NMI $= 0.23$ vs.\ $0.01$, Cram\'{e}r's $V = 0.45$; Appendix~\ref{app:type_mechanism}), validating the taxonomy as a diagnostic tool: each type maps to a dominant failure mechanism (\S\ref{sec:analysis}).
9	
10	\paragraph{Prompt-based mitigation.}
11	Prompt-based mitigation is type-dependent (effective for authorization scope and lexical but not incompleteness; Appendix~\ref{app:mitigation}), reinforcing policy-design over prompt-level fixes.
12
